Next best bet: launch `stage1_v2_bundle.yaml`, but treat it as a short-kill smoke first, not a full bet. I’m switching the design to an explicit per-row style triplet: own reference, same-author positive reference, same-register different-author negative reference. That gives a direct style-axis loss independent of batch composition.

I’d do a cheap 026 before any big scale-up: 1. Keep `v4.3` strict poetry. 2. Add a surface/leakage penalty or filter: - reject/meta-penalize outputs containing “write”, “try”, “following lines”, “reader”, “perspective”, essay headings, etc. - or add a discriminator/classifier loss for verse-vs-prose/instruction leakage. 3. Add harder style supervision: - author/prototype contrastive objective, - batch-hard negatives, - maybe train against own/swap generated-style preference if cheap. 4. Only scale data after 026 proves the objective can turn pathway improvement into clean samples. So: **scale later, objective now.**

> DEVELOPER

Say if I want to break the whole thing up between 10 tiny subagents, what'ss the best structure for them to work on it such that we can merge them together? What are good holistic instructions that will clean up data while also removign bad struff? What shoudl they be looking for?

> AGENT

Best structure: split by **author + split**, not by arbitrary line ranges. That keeps each subagent calibrated to one author’s real style and avoids inconsistent decisions. Use 10 shards like this: 1. `train_poetry_authors_01` 2. `train_poetry_authors_02` 3. `train_poetry_authors_03` 4. `train_poetry_authors_04` 5. `train_poetry_authors_05` 6. `train_poetry_authors_06` 7. `train_poetry_authors_07` 8. `train_poetry_authors_08` 9. `val_test_poetry_all` 10. `probes_and_swap_refs_all` Each subagent should output a JSONL review file, not directly mutate the master dataset. Then one merge script applies decisions deterministically. Recommended decision schema: ```json { "split": "train", "row_index": 123, "author": "emily_dickinson", "decision": "keep|delete|edit", "reason": "meta_instruction|prose_drift|source_artifact|wrong_register|bad_chunk|duplicate|clean", "ref_text_clean": null, "target_text_clean": null, "notes": "short human-readable note" } ``` For `keep`, clean fields are `null`. For `delete`, clean fields are `null`. For `edit`, include cleaned `ref_text_clean` and/or `target_text_clean`. **Holistic Instructions For Each Agent** Your job is to preserve high-quality author-style poetry pairs and remove anything that teaches the adapter bad behavior. Be conservative with deletion when the text is real verse; be aggressive against meta-instruction, prose, source junk, and wrong-register contamination. Review every assigned row as a pair: - `ref_text`: what the adapter conditions on - `target_text`: what the model learns to write - `author` - `split` Choose exactly one: - `keep`: both ref and target are clean poetry/verse for that author. - `edit`: the row is basically good but has removable junk, such as title headings, footnote markers, page numbers, boilerplate, isolated source artifacts, or a bad first/last line. - `delete`: the row would teach the model the wrong behavior. Delete rows with: - Meta-writing/instruction leakage: - “write a poem” - “try to write” - “use the following lines” - “the reader” - “the poet’s perspective” - “this poem is about” - “first attempt” - explanatory classroom/summary language - Prose drift: - essay paragraphs - literary criticism - summaries - biographies - prefaces/intros - “In his work…” - “The author considers…” - long paragraph blocks with no verse lineation - Wrong register: - screenplay dialogue - speaker labels like `BURR`, `HAMILTON`, `SCENE`, `ACT`, `ENTER`, `EXIT` - dramatic dialogue unless it is clearly the author’s poem and not script-like - Source artifacts: - Gutenberg/license material - footnote blocks - page/image captions - URLs - bracketed citation markers - table-of-contents fragments - roman numeral section pages with no real poem content - Bad chunking: - starts/ends mid-sentence in a way that ruins the passage - only a title, heading, fragment, or isolated stanza too short to learn from - accidental concatenation of multiple […]

> DEVELOPER

Should it try to find ones where the styles feel very inconsistent depsite being by the same author?

> DEVELOPER

like remove them

> AGENT

Yes, but with a guardrail: **remove “style-inconsistent” rows only when they are likely bad data, bad chunking, or wrong subgenre, not just because the author is varied.** They should look for same-author rows that feel inconsistent because of: - **Wrong form/register:** essay, prose preface, dramatic scene, song note, criticism, dialogue block. - **Bad source mixing:** a poem by another author slipped under this author. - **Bad chunking:** two unrelated poems concatenated, orphan ending, title plus fragment, prose note glued to verse. - **Translation/editorial voice:** translator intro, editor note, “the poet says…” - **Juvenile/generic modelbait style:** modern instruction-like verse unlike surrounding corpus. - **Outlier surface:** all other rows are compact lyric verse, but this row is a long argumentative prose paragraph. But they should **not** delete just because: - the author has multiple legitimate modes, - early/late poems differ, - a poet sometimes writes dramatic monologue, - line length varies, - one poem is more archaic, plain, religious, comic, or narrative. Good instruction to add: > If a row feels unlike the author, compare it against nearby same-author rows. Delete only if the mismatch is best explained by contamination, prose/editorial material, wrong register, or broken chunking. If it is plausible genuine author variation, keep it. If only a heading or bad wrapper causes the mismatch, edit rather than delete. Decision reason to add: ```json "reason": "style_outlier_bad_data" ``` And maybe: ```json "confidence": "high|medium|low" ``` Only auto-apply `style_outlier_bad_data` deletes when confidence is `high`. Medium/low should go to a human/lead review queue.

SO you're saying a lot of different inputs have the same outputs?

> AGENT

No, mostly the opposite. A lot of rows have the **same input/reference** and **different outputs/targets**. For val/test, the pattern is: ```text same ref_text + target_1 same ref_text + target_2 same ref_text + target_3 ... ``` Example: ```text Christina Rossetti same reference passage -> 100 different Christina Rossetti target passages Emily Dickinson same reference passage -> 85 different Emily Dickinson target passages Matthew Arnold same reference passage -> 82 different Matthew Arnold target passages ``` So the row count looks large, but the conditioning diversity is small. Why this matters: - The model sees many examples of “given this one Rossetti reference, write many Rossetti-ish targets.” - But eval does not strongly test whether it can adapt to many different Rossetti reference passages. - It may overrepresent one reference as “the author style.” For training this can still be useful. For style-conditioning proof, it is weak. We want more unique references per author.

Pair conservatively** For each author: - choose multiple reference poems - choose different target poems - cap pairs per reference - cap pairs per target - avoid same poem as ref/target - avoid one book dominating an author - keep heldout authors and heldout poems cleanly separated Basically v4.5 rules should become default. **5. Our lesson is that dirty scale actively teaches bad behavior. **6. Use LLM audit only after deterministic filters** Cheap agents/LLMs should review the borderline rows, not the whole universe. Good automatic filters can remove most bad rows first. Then LLMs classify: - keep poem - delete prose/editorial/source artifact - edit small heading/footnote - uncertain **My strongest recommendation** Build a **clean poem corpus first**, not another pair dataset. If we only have one shot, I’d do **surface/style objective on v4.5 first**. If we’re planning the serious path to a robust result, then yes: **broader clean corpus is necessary**, but it should be built carefully and not rushed into training.

So what's the best approach to build the significant starting corpus with standardised data?

> AGENT

Best approach: build a **source-manifest-first corpus factory**, not a scraper pile. The starting corpus should be significant, but conservative. I’d aim for **2,500-10,000 clean poem/chunk records**, not 100k noisy rows. **Recommended V0 Strategy** 1. **Use source manifests as the control plane** Every candidate source gets a manifest row before we touch text: - source id - source family: Gutenberg, IA, Wikisource, Standard Ebooks - author name/id - title - year - URL - license/public-domain basis - source risk - status: candidate/fetched/accepted/rejected This prevents “mystery rows.” 2. **Prioritize sources in this order** For V0: - **Standard Ebooks**: high-quality structured text, lower volume. - **Project Gutenberg expanded via Allison Parrish corpus IDs**: high volume, known public-domain lane. - **Wikisource**: good structure, good provenance. - **Internet Archive pre-1930 high-precision subset**: big expansion, but only after OCR checks. IA should not be first for text ingestion. It should be first for **discovery**, then filtered. 3. **Normalize into one canonical corpus schema** Every poem/chunk row should look like: ```json { "corpus_id": "...", "author_id": "...", "author_name": "...", "title": "...", "text": "...", "source_id": "...", "source_name": "...", "source_url": "...", "source_work_title": "...", "source_publication_year": 1895, "license": "public_domain_us", "public_domain_basis": "published_before_1930_us", "clean_sha256": "...", "raw_sha256": "...", "cleaning_version": "poetry_corpus_v0", "flags": [] } ``` 4. **Clean at poem/source level** Do not build training pairs yet. Filters should remove: - TOCs - prefaces - editor notes - footnotes - textual variants/apparatus - HTML/URLs - classroom prompts - OCR junk - prose criticism - duplicate poem text 5. **Make acceptance gates hard** For `poetry_corpus_v0`, I’d require: - 50+ authors if possible, 25 minimum - 25+ clean records for core train authors where possible - 8+ heldout authors with 20+ records - zero exact duplicate clean text - explicit public-domain basis for every row - all probe/heldout rows manually or subagent audited - artifact hit rate below 1% 6. **Then derive pair datasets** Only after the clean corpus is frozen: - `pairs_v5_0_train.jsonl` - `pairs_v5_0_val.jsonl` - `pairs_v5_0_test.jsonl` - balanced probes Pair rules: - ref and target must be different corpus records - cap rows per reference - cap rows per target - cap rows per author - no target duplicates - heldout authors stay out of train **My strongest recommendation** Build V0 from **three lanes in parallel**: 1. Standard Ebooks + existing Gutenberg cleaned authors for high precision. 2. Allison Parrish Gutenberg Poetry Corpus to discover many more Gutenberg poetry-heavy books. 3. IA pre-1930 high-precision candidates as expansion, but […]

> DEVELOPER

could it be that we just need more training or more data?

> AGENT

Yes, but with different odds: **More training: maybe, but not my first bet.** Exp029 improved pathway metrics by changing objective pressure, not by training longer. The LR was already decaying toward near-zero by the end, so simply extending the same 1200-step schedule probably gives diminishing returns unless we also change the schedule: longer run with slower decay, higher floor LR, or staged training. **More data: yes eventually, but not raw scale yet.** The current 18.6k-row corpus is enough to show the adapter can learn a conditioning signal. More data could help author coverage and style diversity, but if we add noisy/weakly styled data we may just dilute the signal. I would only scale data after we have a stronger data spec: clean author-native poems, enough per author, no anthologies/editorial prose, preferably richer author/style contrast. My current ranking: 1. **Best next bet: objective/training schedule.** Keep v5.4 data. Try stronger style pressure with a better schedule: maybe 2k-3k steps, slower LR decay, maybe `style_triplet_weight` around 1.5-2.0, contrastive 0.2, and watch sampled quality. 2. **Second: better negatives / harder style contrast.** The model needs to know “this is Dickinson vs Keats vs Frost,” not just “this is poem-ish.” Hard negatives from similar-era/genre poets may help. 3. **Third: more data, but curated.** Broader source-native corpus is useful, but only if we keep the cleanliness bar. I would not pour in a huge corpus before fixing the style-alignment bottleneck. 4. **Less likely: just more of the same training.** It might improve a bit, but the qualitative issue looks like weak style transfer, not underfit NTL. So: **yes, more training/data could help, but the evidence says “more targeted training pressure” before “more scale.”**

> DEVELOPER

How could we clean them up? Like check do the pairs match style, are they quality in general, are there weirdness and therefore should they be kept/removed/edited. How could a subagent army do this? What woudl their brief look like?

> AGENT

Yes. Best structure: **don’t have agents directly rewrite the canonical JSONL.** Have them produce auditable decision files, then merge deterministically. For 3,859 train pairs, use **10 agents x ~386 pairs**. **Unit Of Work** Each agent gets one shard: ```text pairs_v5_4_train_shard_00.jsonl pairs_v5_4_train_shard_01.jsonl ... pairs_v5_4_train_shard_09.jsonl ``` Each row has `pair_id`, `author`, `ref_text`, `target_text`, source ids/titles. Agent outputs: ```json { "pair_id": "...", "decision": "keep" | "delete" | "edit", "severity": "none" | "minor" | "major", "reasons": ["ocr", "prose", "metadata", "style_mismatch", "weak_poem", "duplicate_like"], "edited_ref_text": null, "edited_target_text": null, "notes": "short reason" } ``` Then a merge script applies: - `keep`: unchanged - `delete`: remove pair - `edit`: replace only the affected text fields - reject invalid edits automatically if empty, too short, or too different from original **Agent Brief** ```text You are auditing source-native poetry training pairs for an author-style adapter. Goal: Decide whether each pair should be kept, deleted, or minimally edited. You will receive JSONL rows with: - author - ref_text - target_text - source ids/titles - pair_id For each row, judge BOTH texts. Keep if: - both ref_text and target_text are real poems or clear poem excerpts - both plausibly belong to the same author/style bucket - no obvious OCR, table of contents, publisher ads, prose criticism, biography, prompt text, title lists, or metadata - minor archaic spelling, dialect, or old punctuation is acceptable Delete if: - either side is mostly prose, preface, notes, criticism, biography, ads, TOC, index, title list, or transcriber matter - either side is badly OCR-corrupted - the style feels obviously not from the same author bucket - the text is generic modern prompt/instruction material - the pair would teach the model to ignore the reference or write non-poetry Edit only if: - the row is basically good but has small removable contamination - examples: page number, isolated title/header/footer, OCR mark, short editorial line - do not modernize style - do not rewrite creatively - do not “improve” poetry - preserve original wording as much as possible Important: Be stricter than generous. Ambiguous bad rows should be deleted. Do not delete merely because the poem is narrative, dialect-heavy, religious, archaic, or not to your taste. The output must be JSONL decisions only. ``` **What They Should Look For** Highest-priority delete signals: - TOC/title lists: many short titles, page numbers, roman numerals - prose wrappers: “published”, “edition”, “transcriber”, “preface”, “introduction” - review/advertising blurbs - biography or criticism about the poet […]

It was good. Not “solved,” but definitely the clearest positive result so far. The subagent pair audit was worth it: - Train pairs dropped from **3,859 → 3,135** - We removed/edited a lot of OCR, prose, metadata, drama/dialogue, and style-mismatch rows - Despite less data, pathway metrics improved hard Best comparison: ```text exp029, before pair audit: K_last_swap = 0.1711 V_last_swap = 0.1470 exp030, after pair audit: K_last_swap = 0.1231 V_last_swap = 0.1048 ``` That’s a real win. It means the bad/incoherent pairs were actively hurting the adapter, and cleaner data beat more data. But sampled outputs still aren’t where we want them. They’re cleaner than baseline/no-ref, but still generic and sometimes prose-ish. So the adapter is learning a better conditioning signal, but decoding/style realization is still weak. My read: **8/10 scientifically promising, 5/10 product-quality output.** Next best bet is not broad data cleanup anymore. Use v5.7 and work on generation-facing style alignment: prompt format, decoding, stronger/direct style objective, or better author-style exemplars.

> DEVELOPER

WHY DON'T YOU THINK IT NEEDS MORE TRAINING?

> AGENT

I don’t think “just more of the same training” is the best first bet because of the LR schedule and the failure shape. In exp030, by the end of 1200 steps the learning rate had decayed basically to zero: - near step 1150, `lr_projector ≈ 4.7e-8` - near step 1175, `lr_projector ≈ 1.2e-8` So extending that exact run would mostly train at a dead LR. It would add time, not much learning. Also, the pathway is already strong now: - `K_last_swap=0.1231` - `V_last_swap=0.1048` - random/code controls low That says the adapter signal is getting through. The remaining problem is the **decoded text**: generic, sometimes prose-ish, weakly author-specific. That smells less like “hasn’t trained enough to route conditioning” and more like “objective/decoding doesn’t force the style to surface.” But I do think more training could help if we change the schedule. The useful version would be: - train longer, but with slower LR decay or LR floor - maybe 2k-3k steps - keep v5.7 data - keep stronger style pressure - maybe reduce NTL dominance or add a generation/style loss So I’m not against more training. I’m against **naively continuing the same 1200-step cosine schedule** and expecting a big jump. A proper longer-run ablation is absolutely worth doing.

Current setup: - `adapter`: text prompt is just `instruction + "\n"`; reference goes through `reference_ids/reference_mask` into the adapter. - `no_ref`: same text prompt, but raw base model, no adapter reference channel. - `prompted_baseline`: raw base model with the reference pasted into the prompt as text. So yes, there are two fair comparisons, but they answer different questions: 1. **Adapter channel effect:** `adapter + instruction only` vs `no_ref + same instruction only` This tests whether the adapter is doing anything. 2. **Product-relevant fair baseline:** `adapter + encoded reference + instruction` vs `base model + reference pasted in prompt + instruction` This tests whether the adapter beats ordinary in-context prompting. You’re right that the second one is the fair “do we beat the obvious baseline?” comparison. We already log it as `prompted_baseline`, but the adapter has an advantage/disadvantage tradeoff: it gets the reference through learned KV, while baseline gets raw readable reference tokens. If the product claim is “use a sample to write in that style,” then `adapter` vs `prompted_baseline` is the comparison that matters most.

> AGENT

Yes. For both **train and adapter eval**, the current approach is: - Reference sample goes into the **adapter encoder only**. - Text prompt gets only: `Write a short poem in the style of the reference passage.\n` - The actual reference text is **not pasted into the LM prompt** for adapter training/eval. That is internally consistent. It trains the adapter to make “reference passage” meaningful through prefix KV, not through visible prompt tokens. Best approach: 1. **Train adapter exactly this way** Keep reference out of the text prompt. Otherwise the base LM can learn to rely on visible reference tokens, and the adapter may become optional/noisy. The clean objective is: `target ~ base_lm(instruction, prefix_kv(reference))` 2. **Eval adapter the same way** Adapter condition should match training: `instruction only + adapter(reference)` 3. **Compare against two baselines** - `no_ref`: same instruction, no adapter, no reference. This measures adapter lift. - `prompted_baseline`: instruction plus reference pasted into prompt, no adapter. This measures whether adapter beats normal in-context prompting. So the fair product comparison is **adapter(reference encoded) vs prompted_baseline(reference pasted)**. The fair mechanism comparison is **adapter vs no_ref**. I would *not* train the adapter with the reference also pasted into the prompt unless we intentionally define a hybrid product: “base sees the sample, adapter also compresses it.” That is a different claim and probably worse for diagnosing the adapter.

Yes, including the reference in the generation prompt would be more consistent with how the base LLM normally uses examples. It would likely improve raw generation quality because the model can directly attend to the sample text. But it changes the claim. Current adapter-only setup asks: `Can the adapter compress the reference into prefix KV well enough that the model writes in that style without seeing the reference text?` Hybrid prompt+adapter setup asks: `Can the adapter improve or stabilize normal in-context style prompting when the model also sees the reference?` For product usefulness, the hybrid setup may be the better target. For scientific diagnosis of the adapter channel, adapter-only is cleaner. My recommendation: - Keep training/eval adapter-only as the **diagnostic core**. - Add a new eval condition immediately: - `prompted_baseline`: reference in prompt, no adapter - `adapter`: reference encoded only - `adapter_prompted`: reference in prompt **and** adapter encoded - If `adapter_prompted > prompted_baseline`, we have a useful product story even if adapter-only remains imperfect. I would not switch training yet. First run eval-only if we have a checkpoint preserved. For the next training run, consider a mixed objective: maybe 70% adapter-only, 30% prompt+adapter, so the adapter still has to carry signal but learns to cooperate with normal prompting.

> DEVELOPER

but what's our best bet at generally improivng the style adherence?

> AGENT

Best bet: **train toward the product setting, not just the clean adapter-only setting.** Right now the adapter has learned to route reference signal, but it often turns that into generic poem-ness. To improve style adherence, I’d do this next: 1. **Use prompt+adapter as a first-class training/eval condition** - Train some fraction of batches with the reference visible in the prompt *and* encoded through the adapter. - Keep some adapter-only batches so the adapter cannot become irrelevant. - Start with something like `70% adapter-only / 30% prompt+adapter`. 2. **Make pair quality more style-specific** - Remove same-author pairs that are only author-matched but not stylistically coherent. - Prefer distinctive, medium-length poems over generic public-domain verse. - Downweight/remove bland children’s/nature verse where many authors collapse into “sun/birds/flowers.” 3. **Add a direct style-ranking objective** - Current triplet helps K/V separation, but not necessarily decoded style. - Add or approximate a loss where output/reference should be closer than output/swap reference using stylometric or judge-derived signals. - Even a cheap post-generation reranker for training/eval could tell us what the model is failing to optimize. 4. **Evaluate against the right baseline** - Main product metric should be `adapter_prompted` vs `prompted_baseline`. - If adapter only helps when the prompt also includes the sample, that’s still useful. The biggest conceptual change: stop expecting compressed KV alone to carry all author style. Let the base model read the sample normally, and train the adapter to **steer, stabilize, and amplify** that style signal.

> DEVELOPER

do we evne know that style adheerence is fundamentally working though, right?

> AGENT

Not fully. We know the **reference channel is working**; we do **not yet know that true style adherence is fundamentally working**. What we have proven: - The adapter encodes different references differently. - The projector no longer collapses globally. - Swap/random/code references produce different K/V. - Adapter outputs are cleaner than `no_ref`. - Exp031 got very strong pathway separation: `K_last_swap=0.050`, `V_last_swap=0.045`. What we have not proven: - Generated text reliably matches the specific author/reference style. - The model beats normal reference-in-prompt generation. - The adapter is learning style rather than broad “poem-ish / archaic / line-broken” features. - The output changes are aligned with the reference instead of just being different. So the honest status is: **Mechanism: yes, strong. Style adherence: partially suggested, not established. Product usefulness: currently unknown until exp032 hybrid comparison finishes.** The next decisive question is exactly exp032: `prompted_baseline(reference in prompt)` vs `adapter_prompted(reference in prompt + adapter)` If `adapter_prompted` clearly improves style match, then style adherence is working in the practical setting. If not, we only have a good conditioning mechanism, not a solved style adapter.

> DEVELOPER

What are the best bets at improivng it beyond this?

> AGENT

I’ll answer that directly, and I’m also keeping the exp032 run attached in the background. The next bets should be aimed at proving and improving decoded style adherence, not just pushing pathway metrics lower.

> AGENT

Best bets, in priority order: 1. **Add a real style-adherence evaluator** Right now pathway metrics are ahead of our judgment. We need a cheap, repeatable score that asks: “Which output better matches this reference style, A or B?” for `adapter_prompted` vs `prompted_baseline`, `adapter` vs `no_ref`, and `adapter` vs `adapter_swap`. Without this, we keep optimizing proxies. 2. **Train/eval in the product setting** If users will provide a style sample in the prompt, train a hybrid condition: - some batches: adapter-only - some batches: prompt+adapter - maybe some prompt-only baseline eval This tests whether the adapter can amplify normal in-context style matching instead of replacing it. 3. **Curate for distinctive style, not just clean poetry** The corpus is cleaner, but many rows are still generic public-domain poem substance: birds, flowers, moral reflection, childlike rhymes. Same-author pairs can be “valid” but stylistically weak. We should select rows with strong author signatures and remove bland/generic pairs even if they are technically poems. 4. **Improve pair construction** Current pair target is: same author reference -> same author target. But same-author texts can vary wildly by period, form, dialect, translation, anthology source, or OCR quality. Better pair construction: - same author, same substyle/form when possible - medium-length refs and targets - avoid one prose-ish ref paired with one lyric target - avoid “dedication/footer/title-heavy” chunks - choose hard negative swaps from nearby authors/forms 5. **Add output-level style pressure** The current triplet separates encoded references/KV, not decoded outputs. A stronger bet is to add an auxiliary generated-output evaluator/reranker, even offline at first: - generate candidates - judge/stylometric score against reference vs swap - use this to choose data, tune decoding, or later train preference-style objective 6. **Fix prompt format and decoding around the intended product** The current instruction is generic and may encourage generic poetry. For hybrid, prompt should be closer to the real task: `Use the reference only for style: diction, rhythm, lineation, syntax. Write a new poem. Do not explain.` Then compare prompt-only vs prompt+adapter under identical text prompt. My strongest bet: **hybrid training + stricter distinctive-style data selection + pairwise style judge eval**. Data alone helps, training time helps pathway, but style adherence needs the objective/eval to look at style, not just K/V difference.

> DEVELOPER

wait, how does that work? Is that a asmart way to handle this/

> AGENT

It’s a **useful first-pass diagnostic**, but not smart enough to be the final filter by itself. What I tried was: - Represent each poem chunk by character n-gram style features. - For each chunk, compare: - similarity to other chunks by the **same author** - similarity to chunks/prototypes from **other authors** - Keep chunks where: - same-author similarity beats nearest-other-author similarity - length is medium - generic-word density is not too high - no obvious prose/artifact cues - Then keep pairs where **both ref and target pass**, and ref/target are not wildly dissimilar. The problem: a global cutoff like `author_advantage >= 0.015` was too harsh. It kept only 55 train pairs after author-count gating. That does **not** mean only 55 good pairs exist. It means this metric is conservative and biased toward authors with very distinctive spelling/diction/form. Better strategy: 1. **Use this score for ranking, not hard global filtering** - For each author, rank their candidate pairs by: - ref/target medium length - ref-target cohesion - low artifact/prose risk - high within-author distinctiveness - Keep the top N per author, not only rows above one global threshold. 2. **Use buckets** - `strong_style`: top 20-30% within author - `medium_style`: next 30-40% - `weak/generic`: discard or downweight - This avoids deleting authors whose style is real but subtler by the metric. 3. **Evaluate pairs with multiple signals** A pair is good if: - both poems are clean - both are actually poems - both are medium length - ref and target feel like same author/form/register - neither is generic public-domain mush - target does not copy ref too closely - same-author similarity beats plausible negative authors 4. **Use LLM audit on borderline/high-impact cases** The cheap metric should triage. A small judge/agent audit should decide: - “keep” - “delete” - “edit/trim” - “stylistically incoherent same-author pair” So: **smart as a scoring feature, not smart as the whole decision rule**. The better filtering policy is: **within-author top-ranked distinctive/coherent pairs**, with a minimum author count, plus manual/LLM audit for the retained training set. That should give us maybe hundreds to low-thousands of much better pairs, instead of nuking the corpus down to 55.

> DEVELOPER

Why not just do an LLM filtering on all?

> AGENT

We can. At this scale, **LLM filtering all pairs is probably the right move**. The reason I started with cheap scoring is that it gives structure: it tells the LLM what to pay attention to and gives us sortable diagnostics. But with only ~3,135 train pairs, full LLM audit is feasible and likely better than relying on stylometry. Best setup: - One LLM decision per pair. - Output strict JSONL: - `decision`: `keep`, `delete`, `edit` - `style_strength`: `strong`, `medium`, `weak` - `pair_coherence`: `high`, `medium`, `low` - `artifact_risk`: `none`, `minor`, `major` - `reason` - optional cleaned `ref_text` / `target_text` - Keep only: - `decision=keep` - `style_strength in {strong, medium}` - `pair_coherence in {high, medium}` - `artifact_risk != major` The key is the brief. It should not merely ask “is this poetry?” It should ask: > Would training on this ref-target pair teach a model to imitate a coherent, distinctive author style? Delete if: - the pair is bland/generic poem language - ref and target feel like different modes/forms despite same author - either side is prose, OCR, metadata, title/dedication-heavy, drama/dialogue, translation weirdness, or prompt-like text - the author signal is weak enough that many poets could have written it - the target mostly copies the reference Keep if: - both sides are clean poems - medium length - same authorial voice/form/register - style is at least moderately distinctive - target is different content but similar style So yes: **LLM filter all pairs**, with cheap metrics as context, then build v5.9 from the kept medium/strong pairs. That’s our best data-side bet.

> DEVELOPER

Why do you think there's no value in more steps?

> AGENT

I don’t think there is **zero** value in more steps. I think the evidence says **more steps on this exact recipe is a low-probability lever**. Why: 1. **Loss is flat** From 0-5k continuation, loss stayed around `3.7-3.8`, with no clear downward trend. So the model is not obviously still climbing a useful learning curve. 2. **More steps already got tested** Exp036 pushed Gemma4 from 2k to about 10k total. It did not monotonically improve. Some later outputs became more poem-like, but own-reference binding stayed weak. 3. **The key metric does not improve with steps** In exp037: - step 1000 own-vs-swap: `0.417 / +0.023` - step 2000: `0.333 / -0.019` - step 3000: `0.417 / -0.025` - step 4000: `0.667 / -0.011` - final: `0.417 / -0.024` That is noise, not a learning trend. If it were learning reference-specific style, own-reference should increasingly beat swapped-reference. 4. **The failures look data/format-like** Samples still say things like “write another poem,” produce essay answers, analysis, HTML/classroom artifacts, or generic sentimental verse. More optimization can reinforce those artifacts instead of fixing them. 5. **Prompt+adapter improves more than adapter specificity** The adapter often helps versus prompt-only, but swapped-reference often works similarly. That implies it learned a generic poetry prior, not a reliable style key. So my view is: **more steps could slightly polish outputs, but it probably won’t unlock style adherence unless we fix the target distribution and eval setup.** If we do run more training, I’d only do it after a cleaner corpus/prompt-format pass, or as a small controlled test from the best checkpoint rather than another long blind blast.

> DEVELOPER

And if you could guess what the root of the problem is here, what would you say that it is? Improving everything or some kind of fundamental misconception or misapprehension?

> AGENT

My guess: **not a fundamental impossibility, but a target/signal mismatch.** The adapter is probably learning “this is poetry-ish text” more than “this reference has a specific reusable style signature.” Root causes, in order: 1. **Training pairs may not demand reference-specific transfer** Many targets can be satisfied by generic poem/prose behavior. If the target does not strongly depend on the exact reference style, the adapter has no reason to encode fine style identity. 2. **The corpus still contains task-answering contamination** Outputs like essays, explanations, “write another…”, HTML/classroom artifacts mean the model is partly learning “respond to an instruction dataset,” not “emit pure stylized text.” 3. **Author labels/styles are too diffuse** “Same author” may include very different modes, genres, periods, or source quirks. The model sees inconsistent style under one implied key. 4. **The eval prompt may be doing too much** Prompt+adapter sometimes wins, but own-reference does not beat swapped-reference. That suggests the base prompt is carrying most of the generation behavior, while adapter signal is weak or generic. 5. **We may be asking a small prefix adapter to learn from a weak supervisory signal** If the loss is mostly next-token target imitation, and references/targets are only loosely stylistically coupled, the easiest solution is generic poetry fluency, not style discrimination. So I’d frame it as: **we’re not failing because style adapters can’t work; we’re failing because the data/eval objective does not force the model to care which style reference it got.** Best next move: create a smaller but much sharper corpus where each pair is: - pure output text, no explanation/task residue - medium/high distinctive style - reference and target clearly stylistically compatible - swapped references obviously wrong - eval explicitly checks own-reference vs hard negative references If that still fails, then we start suspecting architecture/objective limits. Right now, I think the data/objective signal is the main culprit.

> AGENT

Because the split is very sharp: - **Adapter-only works best at step 1600** - own reference beats swapped reference: `0.667 / +0.128` - adapter beats no-ref: `0.583 / +0.076` - **Prompt+adapter is bad at the same checkpoint** - adapter+prompt vs prompt-only: `0.167 / -0.158` That pattern means the adapter is carrying some reference-specific signal, but when we also include the generation prompt, the output quality/style score gets worse relative to prompt-only. Why I suspect prompt+adapter specifically: 1. **The prompt may activate instruction-answering behavior** Samples include “Student’s response,” “The reference is,” “In response to the instruction,” “Solution,” analysis prose. That looks like prompt/task contamination, not just weak style. 2. **Prompt-only is often already strong/generic** A base LM with a prompt can write plausible generic poetry. Adding a noisy adapter can perturb it, so the pairwise judge may prefer prompt-only. 3. **Adapter-only isolates the reference channel** In adapter-only, own-vs-swap asks: does this reference produce a better style than another reference? At 1600, yes. So the reference channel is not dead. 4. **Prompt+adapter tests a different thing** It asks: does adapter improve a prompted generation over a strong prompt baseline? That mixes reference specificity, instruction following, base-model prompt priors, and contamination. It may be a harsher and less diagnostic test. So my hypothesis is: **the adapter has begun learning style, but our prompt format is fighting or drowning that signal.** Next test should compare several prompt formats, especially much shorter prompts, and maybe an adapter-only continuation-style eval.

Is this just next token completion model now? What makes snense in light of that?

> AGENT

Yes. With `google/gemma-3-4b-pt`, we should treat it as a **next-token completion model**, not an instruction-following model. That changes the right approach pretty materially. What makes sense now: 1. **Stop using instruction-like eval prompts** Prompts like “write a poem…” or “in the style of…” can trigger weird pretrained web/task patterns: worksheets, explanations, “Student response,” HTML, analysis. A PT model is completing the prompt, not obeying it. 2. **Make train/eval look like pure continuation** Better format is closer to: ```text [reference style text] [new poem begins] ``` Then the model simply continues in the latent-conditioned style. No “write,” no “style,” no “reference is,” no classroom phrasing. 3. **Judge adapter-only / minimal-prefix first** Step 1600 suggests the adapter channel works when it is not competing with an instruction prompt. So the primary eval should be: - same neutral continuation stub - own adapter vs swapped adapter - no verbose prompt 4. **Clean data for completion purity** Remove targets containing: - “The reference is” - “Solution” - “Explanation/Analysis” - “Student response” - HTML tags - “write a poem” - copied famous poems - essay/question-answer artifacts 5. **Train around the 1600-step regime** More to 2400 degraded style binding. So next run should use checkpoint evals around `1200/1600/2000`, with the cleaner completion-style data/eval. So the root correction is: **for Gemma3-PT, do not ask it to follow instructions. Set up a distribution where the next natural tokens are the desired poem, and let the adapter bias that continuation.** Step 1600 is encouraging exactly because it says the adapter can steer completion. The prompt+adapter failure probably means our prompt was shaped like an instruction dataset, which is poison for this base-model setup.

> AGENT

Yes, that makes sense. The key is: **do not ask it to “write a poem.” Give it a document pattern where the natural next-token completion is the second sample.** Something like: ```text Here is a piece of writing by an author. [reference text] Here is another piece of writing by the same author. ``` Then generation begins immediately after that. The target is the second piece. This is better than “continue the poem” because it tells the base model the reference is context, not the text to continue. And it fits a pretrained completion model: it is completing a corpus-like document, not obeying an instruction. For eval, the fair comparison should be: - **prompt-only:** same scaffold + reference text, no adapter - **prompt+own-adapter:** same scaffold + reference text + own adapter - **prompt+swapped-adapter:** same scaffold + reference text + wrong adapter - maybe **adapter-only with tiny scaffold:** no reference in prompt, own adapter only The crucial question becomes: > Given the same visible reference prompt, does the adapter improve the second-piece completion, and does the own adapter beat a swapped adapter? That is a much fairer “impact of adapter” test than our current instruction prompt. It also prevents the adapter from being evaluated in an artificial vacuum. I’d make the scaffold very plain and non-instructional, for example: ```text A piece of writing: {reference} Another piece by the same writer: ``` Avoid “please,” “observe,” “style,” “write,” “interesting,” “authorial voice,” etc. Those words invite analysis/instruction behavior. The PT model should just see a pattern and continue it. Best next experiment: reuse Gemma3-4B-pt, train/eval in this paired-completion format, checkpoint around `1200/1600/2000`.
